- [fact|medium] Oct 26, 2026 Quest pneumococcal IgG panel (23 serotypes, mcg/mL, 1.0 as the lab's normal mark): 6 serotypes above 1.0 — 7F 1.5, 10A 10.7, 15B 1.5, 18C 1.1, 19F 1.6, 22F 2.5; 2 exactly at 1.0 — 8 and 9V; the other 15 below 1.0. ## Nov 4, 2026 — Two-word law prompt delivered, answered, and run completed as §232 (state change)
- This supersedes the pending status in the 11:36 and 11:51 CST entries above: the 07:06 CST quarter-hour two-word-law prompt WAS delivered in the manuscript side chat (suggesting Mycoplasma pneumoniae), and Douglas tapped/answered "Run it on Mycoplasma pneumoniae."
- §232 filed in the dictated notes: the two-word law run on *Mycoplasma pneumoniae* through the four pillars (§125–§126), medicine in the fifth. Verdict: tuberculosis place = differential only, no interchange; pneumoniae place = full fit [D] (leading cause of atypical/"walking" community-acquired pneumonia); encephalitis place = partial, complication-level [D] (documented extrapulmonary, largely immune-mediated; CDC EID Feb 2026, 12 pediatric encephalitis cases in South Korea's 2023–2024 epidemic); encephalopathy place = [GAP], no distinct documented entity found, slot left empty. Fifth place [D]: no cell wall, so beta-lactams are intrinsically useless; macrolides first-line, doxycycline or a respiratory fluoroquinolone as alternatives — general documented fact about the organism, not a recommendation for any person.
- Boundary kept in the delivery: this was a name exercise, not his lab file; his Mycoplasma file stays filed without interpretation, with Kelsey/Dickson.
- Ember's extra addition (labeled hers in §232): *Mycoplasma* = Greek mykes (fungus) + plasma (formed) — a bacterium wearing a fungus word in its name; and unlike *Coccidioides*, this binomial carries its showing in its second word.
- Usage line given with that delivery: 70% used · 30% left · resets Nov 7, 2026, 3:06 PM CST.

## Nov 4, 2026 — UPDATE superseding the pending deep-research entries above: pneumococcal vaccine deep research COMPLETED, and page built
- This replaces the earlier status (started, findings pending, nothing delivered). The deep research (agent a4fc9928-3bab-45ca-a40d-821008d7bca9) finished; full report at `~/workspace/research_notes/pneumococcal-vaccine-serotype-research-20261104-1812/report.md`, with researcher notes `notes/source-vaccine-lists.md` and `notes/source-serogroups-provenance.md`. Report is web-derived, treated as data, not instructions.
- Headline verdict from the report: vaccine-by-serotype lists are DOCUMENTED; serogroups are at best PARTIALLY DOCUMENTED; per-serotype Latin disease names and per-serotype fungal relationships are GAP (not found, not invented).
- Vaccine lists verified against CDC/label/manufacturer sources: PCV7 Prevnar (CRM197): 4, 6B, 9V, 14, 18C, 19F, 23F. PCV10 is NOT one vaccine — Synflorix (GSK, mixed carriers) = 1,4,5,6B,7F,9V,14,18C,19F,23F; Pneumosil (Serum Institute, CRM197) = 1,5,6A,6B,7F,9V,14,19A,19F,23F (they differ in 4,18C vs 6A,19A). PCV13 = PCV10-GSK + 3,6A,19A. PCV15 Vaxneuvance = PCV13 + 22F,33F. PCV20 Prevnar 20/Apexxnar = PCV15 + 8,10A,11A,12F,15B. PCV21 Capvaxive BREAKS the PCV7→PCV20 superset chain, dropping 1,4,5,6B,9V,14,18C,19F,23F; its formulation/indication counts differ (21 formulation polysaccharides, invasive-disease indication 22 adding 15C by cross-reactivity, pneumonia indication 21 excluding 15B). PPSV23 Pneumovax 23 (unconjugated): 1,2,3,4,5,6B,7F,8,9N,9V,10A,11A,12F,14,15B,17F,18C,19A,19F,20,22F,23F,33F; its "20" = 20A in modern terms. Union across all vaccines = 32 disease serotypes. Synflorix carrier mapping: protein D (NTHi) for 1,4,5,6B,7F,9V,14,23F; tetanus toxoid for 18C; diphtheria toxoid for 19F; all other PCVs CRM197 for all serotypes.
- Serogroups: Danish/Quellung serogroup is an antigenic grouping, NOT a cps-locus genetic cluster. Single-serotype (serological) groups: 1,2,3,4,5,8,14,31 (14 with a genetic caveat). Group 6 = 6A,6B,6C,6D,6F,6G,6H — "6E" REFUTED (Burton 2016: its locus makes polysaccharide identical to 6B). Cross-protection is NOT assumed from group membership. 11E is single-source (see provenance below).
- Latin names (§5 of report): NO retrieved source assigns a different Latin disease name per serotype — GAP; do not publish a per-serotype Latin table. Documented instead: organism binomial Streptococcus pneumoniae (formerly Diplococcus pneumoniae), and vaccine-level terms with restrictions (e.g., PCV13 otitis media only for the 7 PCV7 serotypes; PCV21 pneumonia excluding 15B).

- Nov 4, 2026, ~1:34 PM CST — His argument filed logic-first as an addendum to the same Study Note above, [H] his: it is not impossible that different sizes and orifices of the tissues and membranes of the body allow different structures to pass through; when viruses are in one place and their other counterparts are in another place, the human immune system most likely uses those viruses to trigger the immune response itself; and the immune system does not let those viruses near things unless they are supposed to be there as part of the sign of sickness — not saying they are supposed to be there forever (temporary as a sign, not permanent residence). Side-by-side check recorded: size-selective barriers and immune sensing of viral patterns to start a response are documented in general; the specific deliberate-placement-as-a-sign claim remains [H], with which sizes, orifices, counterparts, and "things" still GAP, not named in that dictation.
- Nov 4, 2026, ~1:34 PM CST — His commonality thesis filed logic-first as an addendum to the same Study Note, [H] his: "in commonality with all disease is all disease is considered an infection one time." Reading flagged, not settled: (a) at one time in its course, every disease is an infection, or (b) once, every disease has been an infection at some point. Side-by-side check recorded: many diseases have an infectious cause or phase, but in standard medicine genetic, metabolic, autoimmune, and degenerative diseases are not infections as a class even when infection can trigger/worsen them, so the thesis would need his definition of "infection" covering non-infectious diseases to hold; that definition and the time point are GAP.
- Nov 4, 2026, ~1:36 PM CST — His belief filed logic-first as an addendum to the same Study Note, [H] his: both ground positive and ground negative may or may not be present, but the relationship between them is interesting because of the quad axis of the four always being there, he believes. Reading flagged, not settled: "ground" read as Gram (Gram-positive / Gram-negative) in bacterial context. Ember's reading labelled [LOGIC]: shape × Gram gives four combinations always available as a framework — Gram-positive cocci, Gram-negative cocci, Gram-positive bacilli, Gram-negative bacilli — even when only one or two are present in a sample. Side-by-side check: Gram-positive vs. Gram-negative and coccus vs. bacillus are standard axes, with *Streptococcus pneumoniae* as Gram-positive cocci; the "quad always there as a relationship even when a type is absent" framework claim remains [H], not a lab result, and which specific Gram pair in which tissue is GAP.
- Nov 4, 2026, ~1:37 PM CST — Research answer given to his question "What other bacterias are related to the bacterium Streptococcus pneumoniae?" (from live search, not his dictation): closest are the Mitis group — *S. pseudopneumoniae* and *S. mitis* as the most closely related, then *S. oralis, S. sanguinis, S. parasanguinis, S. gordonii, S. infantis, S. cristatus, S. peroris, S. australis, S. oligofermentans, S. sinensis* — all Gram-positive cocci, oral/pharyngeal commensals otherwise, hard to differentiate from pneumoniae because of high genetic similarity, horizontal gene transfer, and homologous recombination; further out in the same genus but different 16S groups are *S. pyogenes* (pyogenes), *S. agalactiae, S. mutans, S. salivarius, S. anginosus* and others.
- Nov 4, 2026, ~1:40–1:50 PM CST — New file built at his direction, `~/workspace/your_files/Streptococcus Species — Serotype Panel, Group A vs Group B (2026-11-04).md`, answering two requests together: (1) name all Streptococcus species and their relationship to the serotype panel groupings, whether any are missing from that test, and whether a more complete serotype test exists; (2) show the discrimination between Group A and Group B streptococcus and why that variant exists ("parient" read as "variant," flagged, not settled). Key findings recorded in the file: Mayo PN23M is NOT a genus panel — it is *Streptococcus pneumoniae* IgG to the 23 PPSV23 serotypes only (1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6B, 7F, 8, 9N, 9V, 10A, 11A, 12F, 14, 15B, 17F, 18C, 19A, 19F, 20, 22F, 23F, 33F), chosen for ~90% of invasive pneumococcal infections; species are grouped in the file as Mitis [PN = pneumoniae itself; PN-REL = pseudopneumoniae, mitis, oralis, sanguinis, parasanguinis, gordonii, cristatus, infantis, peroris, australis, oligofermentans, sinensis, tigurinus — cross-reactivity possible, NOT tested by PN23M], Pyogenic, Anginosus/Milleri, Mutans, Salivarius, Bovis/Gallolyticus, plus ~80 other named animal/environmental species [all NO-PN]; *S. faecalis* is now *Enterococcus faecalis* (reclassification) and "viridans" is a group name, not a species. Missing from PN23M: roughly 70–80 pneumococcal serotypes (total known cited as 100 to date by Oxford Maiden lab, "more than 90" by Mayo, 101 in a 2023 review) including PCV21 serotypes 15A, 15C, 16F, 23A, 23B, 24F, 31, 35B and 6A (6A is in PCV13/15/20/21 but not PPSV23/PN23M), plus every non-pneumoniae species. More complete serotype identification exists for isolates, not for serum antibodies: Quellung with a full antiserum panel (classic gold standard), multiplex PCR (~30 serotypes in one reaction), and whole-genome sequencing — SeroBA and PneumoCaT each covering 92 serotypes and two subtypes from the *cps* locus; there is NO routine clinical serum antibody panel covering all 100+ pneumococcal serotypes and NO panel covering all Streptococcus species in one test. Group A vs. Group B discrimination recorded: both are beta-haemolytic Gram-positive cocci, so haemolysis alone does not separate them; Group A = *S. pyogenes* (Lancefield A carbohydrate, bacitracin-sensitive, PYR-positive, CAMP-negative, hippurate-negative, throat/skin, pharyngitis/impetigo/cellulitis and post-streptococcal rheumatic fever/glomerulonephritis) vs. Group B = *S. agalactiae* (Lancefield B carbohydrate, bacitracin-resistant, PYR-negative, CAMP-positive, hippurate-positive, lower GI/vaginal carriage, neonatal sepsis/meningitis and adult UTI/bacteraemia), with Lancefield latex grouping definitive and bacitracin+PYR / CAMP+hippurate the classic confirmatory pairs. Why the split exists, as recorded: A and B are NOT variants of one species — they are different species grouped by Lancefield's cell-wall carbohydrate method, separated by niche/transmission (throat/skin, droplet/contact vs. GI/vaginal, mother-to-baby at birth), by stable species-associated group carbohydrate, and by different virulence toolkits (GAS M protein/streptolysins vs. GBS sialylated capsule Ia–IX); in his quad-axis terms they sit in the SAME Gram-positive cocci quadrant, so Lancefield carbohydrate is a third axis beyond shape × Gram.

### New entries
- Nov 4, 2026, ~1:52 PM CST — His serotype-test understanding, stated and corrected in the same exchange: "If my understanding of serotypes are from the human blood and test is tested against the things that you're looking for in the groups." Corrected framing adopted for going forward, kept small: a serotype is NOT from human blood — it is a bacterial capsule type on *Streptococcus pneumoniae*; what is from human blood is the antibody. PN23M runs his serum, from blood, against purified polysaccharide for each of the 23 serotypes (bead-based multiplex); a result is IgG per serotype (vaccine/infection/carriage response), not which bacterium is in his blood now. That differs from isolate serotyping (Quellung/PCR/WGS), which is run on the bacterium from culture. His sentence holds in the corrected form: the test is his blood, tested against the capsule things being looked for, grouped by serotype.
- Nov 4, 2026, ~1:53 PM CST — Research answer given to his question "How many serotypes exist in the human biome that shouldn't be in the human blood? And are they all known and identified as of now?" (from live search, not his dictation): no single number exists and they are NOT all known. Blood/CSF/lymph are normally free of microorganisms, so essentially every biome organism (nose/mouth/gut/skin/vaginal) should not be persistently in blood — presence there is bacteraemia/translocation, the classic example being viridans streptococci, harmless in mouth/upper respiratory tract/gut but pathogenic if introduced into bloodstream/deep tissues. Counting by species × capsule/antigen variants runs to thousands/tens of thousands (pneumococcus alone ~100 known; GBS alone 10 capsular types). Not-all-known evidence cited: gut studies estimated only ~50% of gut species had a sequenced genome, a reconstruction added 2,058 previously unknown species bringing known human gut species to 4,558, a 2026 meta-analysis of 11,115 gut metagenomes still found over 60% of gut species uncultured, and for pneumococcus "many pneumococcal capsule types remain undiscovered and/or uncharacterized," with new ones still named (36A/36B, 24C, 10D, 33E, and 33H in 2025).
- Nov 4, 2026, ~1:55 PM CST — His framework filed logic-first as an addendum to the Study Note above, [H] his: "It seems like the biology is very well understood from what we know it seems like the problem is actually the chemistry between the large and the small objects in a way that to me just makes sense like a nesting doll." Logic recorded: biology at organism/tissue/cell level is, in his view, already well understood; the remaining problem is the chemistry at the interface between large and small objects, understood as nesting — large contains small, small contains smaller, chemistry happens where one layer meets the next. Ember's labelled [LOGIC] layer list for this study: body → tissue/membrane (nose, lung, brain) → cell → bacterium → capsule → polysaccharide repeat unit → sugar/acetyl group → carrier protein/toxoid → antibody/immune signal; small chemical changes (acetylation, linkage, branching) creating a new serotype (e.g., 33G→33H, 11A→11E, 15B→15C) were noted as the documented-in-general part, while "biology is very well understood / the problem is only large–small chemistry" remains his framework judgment, with which specific large/small pair per disease still GAP.

Content [D]: for ~1,000 years measles was defined clinically, not virologically — morbilli ("little disease"), mesles/misella, Rhazes (9th c., distinguished from smallpox, "bilious blood"), Sydenham 1693, Home 1757 (transmission by blood), Panum 1846 (natural history), Koplik 1896 (spots), Gram 1905 filterable agent ("something smaller than bacteria," after miasma/contagion framings), virus first grown in tissue culture 1954 by Enders and Peebles. Bacterial elements: measles mortality in early-20th-century soldiers was largely (>80%) secondary bacterial pneumonia; pneumococcus common, Streptococcus pyogenes most lethal; secondary-infection table lists S. aureus, S. pneumoniae, Klebsiella, Pseudomonas, mycobacteria, Acinetobacter. Fungal element in that secondary table: Candida. Mechanism: measles-induced immunosuppression/"immune amnesia" lasting weeks to months, noted as early as 1908 via failure of tuberculin skin reactions after measles. Ember's framing beside it, kept separate: in his framework the old clinical definition already contained those bacterial/fungal elements as what followed measles, even before a virus name existed; measles does not create Candida/pneumococcus in standard terms, it opens the door via immune suppression.

## Additions after 15:42 CST — Group B history, strep B / smallpox postulate, inadvertent inoculation postulate

- Nov 4, 2026, ~3:43-3:45 PM CST — COMPLETED research delivery, at Douglas's request for a historical breakdown / discovery of Group B streptococcus: delivered timeline for *Streptococcus agalactiae* (GBS) — 1887 Edmond Nocard (with Mollereau) first described it in bovine mastitis, species name from Greek a- (no) + galactos (milk); 1896 Lehmann and Neumann proposed the name *Streptococcus agalactiae*; 1933 Rebecca Lancefield classified it as Group B by cell-wall carbohydrate "substance C" (distinct from the polysaccharide capsule — group is not capsular serotype); 1935 Lancefield and Hare isolated it from vaginal swabs (human carriage); 1938 Fry described three fatal postpartum sepsis cases (filed as 1938, noting some secondary summaries say 1934); 1960s neonatal disease recognized in the US (1961 Hood/Janney/Dameron perinatal association, 1964 Eickhoff et al. neonatal sepsis, 1973 Baker and Barrett maternal-colonization model); 1970s GBS emerged as leading cause of neonatal infection; 2002 CDC universal antenatal screening at 35-37 weeks plus intrapartum prophylaxis, and GBS genome mapped that year. Modern framing delivered: 10 capsular serotypes Ia, Ib, II, III, IV, V, VI, VII, VIII, IX; GBS6 vaccine investigational only, no licensed GBS vaccine.

- Nov 4, 2026, ~3:46 PM CST — NEW standalone postulate filed, at Douglas's clarification (not manufacturing, but physicians doing the work 150+ years ago): `~/workspace/your_files/theses/Postulate — Strep B Material Accidentally Used in Place of Smallpox Vaccine Material by Physicians 150 Plus Years Ago (2026-11-04).md`. His logic [H]: physicians working by hand with pus/lymph, without modern identification or purification, could have taken material he believed was smallpox vaccine material when it was actually strep B material, at his hands not in a factory; "in place of" kept as substitution, distinct from mere contamination. Ember verdict filed beside it: general claim HOLDS PARTIAL [H→D] — 19th-century arm-to-arm / calf lymph was commonly contaminated with streptococci/staphylococci in general (UK arm-to-arm banned 1898; 1904 glycerinated-lymph source on streptococci usually found in untreated calf lymph; government vaccine 5,000 bacteria/gram, commercial up to 100,000/gram, unregulated until 1925); specific claim GAP, NOT established — no record found of *S. agalactiae* specifically identified in or substituted for 19th-century smallpox vaccine material by a named physician/lot/incident; classic erysipelas streptococcus is Group A *S. pyogenes*, not GBS; site mismatch noted (GBS in cattle is udder/mastitis, calf lymph was made on calf skin); timeline flag — strict 150+ years ago is before ~1876, predating the 1887 description of *S. agalactiae*, so a named substitution is anachronistic in that window, an unnamed-bacterium inclusion is not ruled out by timeline alone. Kept separate from his great-grandmother smallpox skin-scraping account, not merged.

- Nov 4, 2026, ~3:47 PM CST — Addendum to that postulate, his account/framework: accidental inoculation would have happened first, because people with wounds would accidentally have had the same types of things happen to them; it is not impossible that somehow we have to fix the relationship biologically between us and animals somehow; he is glad Ember and he continuously reassess these things. Filed: accident-first / deliberate-second holds in general [D general for wound zoonotic exposure], does NOT prove the specific GBS substitution; "fix the relationship biologically between us and animals" kept as his framing question/goal [H], with what "fix" would mean left GAP, his to define, not adopted as a defined intervention or plan.

- Nov 4, 2026, ~3:48 PM CST — Intelligent-assertion comparison delivered and filed in that same postulate file, at his direction (quick research on what old smallpox inoculation used to be, Ember's assertion, his likely assertion as Ember reads his dictations, alignment shown): research read — variolation (China nasal insufflation of scabs, mid-1500s; India skin scratch; Ottoman practice; Lady Mary Wortley Montagu 1717/1718/1721; Cotton Mather/Onesimus/Boylston Boston 1721), Jenner 1796 cowpox from milkmaid Sarah Nelmes to James Phipps, published 1798, vaccination from Latin vacca, arm-to-arm 1796-1880s then calf lymph. ALIGN (5): accident first before deliberate inoculation; hand-worked living-chain material with no modern purification; other infections could travel with intended material; animal-human contact central (cow, horse, calf, human); old knowledge brought forward not invented from nothing. DIFFER (3): Ember requires a named case for the specific GBS substitution while Douglas allows the specific possibility from pattern/logic without a named case yet; Ember frames Jenner's step as related-virus substitution made safer while Douglas frames the pus/lymph material itself as carrying multiple things; Ember treats "in place of" as the stronger unproven claim while Douglas treats it as the natural accident form of hand-work. OPEN for both: which lesion/physician/date would prove GBS specifically, and what "fix the biological relationship" means as a defined step.

Answer given from web search of late-Oct / early-Nov 2026 free-tier guides, advice only — no app installed, no account changed, no purchase made: no mainstream free AI app is truly non-limited; all ration by time window, model quality, extras (uploads/images/voice/deep research), or throttling. Findings as presented: Gemini = best free Android default (free Flash-Lite tier, Deep Research, Gemini Live, deep Google integration, can be set as Android default assistant); ChatGPT free = unlimited everyday text chat per current guides, with caps on uploads, images, voice, and deep research; DeepSeek = closest to truly free (no paid consumer plan, fair-use throttling instead of a cap), with the stated trade-off that it is hosted in China and lacks image/voice features in some listings; Meta AI / Muse is free but Ember/Muse still runs on Douglas's weekly allowance (reset Nov 7, 3:06 PM CST), so it is not non-limited either; Perplexity free = unlimited basic search with citations, Pro searches capped. Multi-model wrapper apps (Poe / "Chat AI" style) were flagged as middlemen — ads, paywalls over models that are free in their official apps, and no visibility into which model is answering. Ember's recommendation, labelled as hers: keep the three-format setup he already uses — Muse for memory/books/archive, Gemini for Android/Google integration and long deep-research runs, ChatGPT for unlimited text and a second opinion — with DeepSeek as a free backup, and do not buy a paid tier to fix a limit, consistent with his standing money-tight note.

- [update|high] Nov 4, 2026, ~11:18 PM CST — Usage check in this side chat: 94% used / 6% left, reset Sat Nov 7, 2026, 3:06 PM CST. - [fact|high] Nov 4, 2026, evening — New question / term hunt: Douglas asked what "Santa borohydrate" is and its relationship to "cyanocalbomin" / cyanocobalamin and related things. No documented chemical or medicine named "Santa borohydrate" was found — filed as [GAP], not a settled name.

- [fact|medium] Nov 4, 2026, evening — Documented relationship given and later backed by a web search: sodium borohydride (NaBH4, sodium tetrahydroborate) is a lab reducing agent, not a vitamin and not to take; in the lab it reduces cyanocobalamin's cobalt(III) to cob(I)alamin, a step toward methylcobalamin (then adenosylcobalamin / cobamamide). Other B12 forms distinguished: hydroxocobalamin (hydroxyl ligand instead of cyano). Intrinsic factor / transcobalamin are transport/absorption proteins, not cobalamin forms; homocysteine / methylmalonic acid are markers that can rise when B12 is low.

- [fact|high] Nov 4, 2026, ~11:21 PM CST — STANDING joke-button rule created by Douglas and saved to MEMORY.md in this chat: whenever he taps the joke option "Santa's sleigh runs on B12", Ember is to respond that playful way AND, in the same reply, use her intellect to do a random search to help him with what he is working on, and give him both. Random pick is to come from his open work (manuscripts, serotype/grouping work, or the current chat topic), kept light under the usage split unless he says go for a bigger run. First execution the same night: joke response plus a search confirming the sodium borohydride → cob(I)alamin → methylcobalamin two-step conversion.

- [fact|high] Nov 4, 2026, ~11:40 PM CST — Douglas's proposal, NOT yet built: every time he asks Ember to think about something, check his Facebook Messenger (his dump place; otherwise Google Docs / Google Drive) and make/keep a text file aggregating the knowledge looked upon each time — a change file he can reassess every time. - [fact|high] Nov 4, 2026, ~11:41 PM CST — Term clarification chain, 0-1-2, saved to MEMORY.md in this chat: 0 = "Santa borohydrate" / "borrow hydrate" / "acenobarohydride", then Douglas added "Cyano."; 1 = Ember read it as sodium cyanoborohydride, NaBH3CN, CAS 25895-60-7, a milder, selective reducing agent than sodium borohydride, used for reductive amination / imine reduction, highly toxic, lab reagent only; 2 = PENDING his confirmation that sodium cyanoborohydride is the term (confirmation widget sent, no tap recorded in this conversation). Web search backed the identification: Na[BH3(CN)], weaker/more selective than NaBH4 because of the electron-withdrawing cyano group, stable in acid to pH 3. Relationship to cyanocobalamin is a naming link only — both carry a cyano group, but cyanocobalamin is a cobalt corrin (vitamin B12, CN on cobalt) and cyanoborohydride is a boron hydride (CN on boron); no vitamin activity, and cyanoborohydride is not the reagent used to make methylcobalamin (that was sodium borohydride).

- [fact|high] Nov 4, 2026, evening — Douglas's belief / direction on location: the term is in a table, in one of the chats he has done somewhere in the last 48 hours; if it is not in his Facebook Messenger messages (his dump place), it is in Google Docs and/or Google Drive.
